The only reason I'm not highlighting the questions on the channels, because when I switch focus, the benchmark gets messed up. But someone's asking, what's the difference between keyed and non-keyed?
[02:58:20.490] And the difference there is that
[02:58:25.290] It's the same reason why you include, if you've ever used React and you've included the key property, it's for this reason.
[02:58:31.260] There's two different ways to look at the DOM when you're rendering in a framework and you're like managing lists.
[02:58:37.200] One way of thinking about it
[02:58:40.950] is...
[02:58:43.380] is that, uh,
[02:58:47.490] Each
[02:58:48.480] like the DOM elements are tied to the data model.  Like they own it.  And what's nice about that approach is that  if the DOM actually persists any state,  like animations or anything, like,
[02:59:00.810] "The Domino's Live and Die."
[02:59:03.060] With
[02:59:04.170] that data model.
[02:59:06.120] And that's why you key, you go like for this ID, these are the DOM elements it owns. If this, you know,
[02:59:12.210] model with this ID goes away, then get rid of those DOM elements. The other view is like, if you have a bunch of DOM elements on the page and you're just changing like you have a list and you're changing which items are at which location, well, instead of
[02:59:24.540] um,
[02:59:26.070] like moving the items physically around.  Like I actually do a better explanation of keyed versus non-keyed,  um,  in a previous stream that I probably have to go dig out.  But like,  like,
[02:59:36.660] The other view is...
[02:59:38.370] You have to understand, sorry, I skipped ahead a bit,  but if you tie the model to the DOM elements,  that means if you move the elements around in the list...
[02:59:46.800] they actually need to move the DOM elements around. But the other view is like, look, I already have a bunch of lists, like items, list items in front of me. If I change the DOM elements, I can just replace the data and update like the data fields and not
[03:00:00.000] move the DOM elements on the page.  And that's what non-keyed is.  You basically kind of like,
[03:00:05.250] When new data comes in, you just hijack the DOM elements that are already present.
[03:00:09.000] Um...
[03:00:09.900] This can be more performant because you're not doing as much sometimes layout calculation or CSS calculation type stuff.
[03:00:18.150] but it means that like if one element was transitioning and now it's on new  data,  like it's,
[03:00:23.700] still transitioning. You don't have that guarantee. There's also other interesting things that are kind of semi-thing, like focus. And things that are related to
[03:00:34.170] The DOM that keeps state that this app state doesn't represent.  Web components are another fun one.  There's like a lot of edge cases here where essentially...
[03:00:43.290] We generally...
[03:00:44.850] recommend that things should be always keyed because you don't want to like
[03:00:49.320] get into this weird zone. And for benchmarks,
[03:00:52.950] Keet is definitely the one that is harder to optimize for.
[03:00:57.330] Because that's why there's like
[03:00:58.920] large list reconciliation functions. If everything was non-keyed, you could literally just like iterate over and like update the values.
[03:01:06.600] You know, and then if the list longer add a few at the end if the list is shorter, you know
[03:01:10.620] drop off the ones on the end.
[03:01:12.690] And but
[03:01:15.510] The hard part is actually...
[03:01:17.520] you know, doing keyed. Like if you actually swap items in keyed, you actually have to like figure out
[03:01:23.910] the least amount of moves. You don't want to bubble sort your DOM when you sort it. You don't want every element to move one at a time.
[03:01:31.470] down the page. You want to do the minimal amount of DOM operations. So, um,
[03:01:35.940] That's what we benchmark here.  So in a sense, I pretty much ignore non-keyed even exists.  When someone releases something and it's only non-keyed,  I don't even register it.  Because it's a cool optimization, but it's not...
[03:01:49.770] terribly practical, but I mean, different frameworks have different opinions here. Obviously, React always, you know, annoyingly goes, you need that key.
[03:01:58.650] Um...
[03:01:59.700] But...
[03:02:00.600] - Yeah.
[03:02:02.040] The...
[03:02:04.350] On the other hand, frameworks like Vue or Svelte
[03:02:10.170] need the key just as much
[03:02:12.930] But they don't actually...
[03:02:15.930] like push you to it.  They're just like,
[03:02:18.150] their default is actually non-keyed.  And then you have to actually opt into a key,  which to me seems like an insane default.  But like...
[03:02:28.020] That's just.
[03:02:29.280] like how they roll. So,
[03:02:32.040] It's interesting to me, definitely, that kind of split.  But I think a lot of that actually has more to do with the easy versus simple mentality and the progressive mentality, less than any technology thing.  I remember someone, I forget, a little while ago was like...
[03:02:48.270] My favorite thing about Svelte is you don't need keys. Sorry to break it to you, that is not true.
[03:02:57.090] You know what I mean?
[03:03:00.240] It leads to misinformation, which makes it harder to teach.
[03:03:05.190] "Keed" is the default for React,
[03:03:07.530] And every VDOM author that I've ever talked to
